The patient denies binge eating and describes strict food restriction resulting in significant weight loss. Since she is post-menopausal, the amenorrhea criterion for F50.0 cannot be traditionally assessed. Both F50.0 (Anorexia nervosa) and F50.1 (Atypical anorexia nervosa) remain candidates. Further evaluation of her fear of weight gain, body image perception, and actual weight status is needed to differentiate between them.", "is_final": false}

I restrict my food intake very carefully, so I never have moments where I lose control like that.\n\nDoctor: Could you tell me your current height and weight, and whether you have recently lost a significant amount of weight?\n\nPatient: I don't know my exact height or weight off the top of my head, but my primary care doctor has been very alarmed by my recent ongoing weight loss. Personally, I feel perfectly fine and think the concern is somewhat overblown, but she insisted I get evaluated.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed your menstrual periods becoming irregular or stopping altogether lately?\n\nPatient: Well, I'm actually 63 years old and went through menopause quite a few years ago. So, I haven't had a menstrual period in a very long time.\n\nDoctor: Do you experience an intense fear of gaining weight or becoming fat, or do you feel that some parts of your body are too fat even though others say you are underweight?\n\nPatient: Yes, if I even consider eating something like pasta or a sandwich, I get incredibly anxious that the weight will show up on my stomach the next morning. My sister constantly tells me I look \"frail\" and that my clothes are hanging off me, but when I look in the mirror, I just see areas where I could stand to tighten up. I really don't see my current weight as a problem at all.\n\nDoctor: Even if you don't know the exact numbers, could you give me a rough estimate of your current weight and height?\n\nPatient: Honestly, I'm really not sure of even a rough estimate for my height or weight. I don't focus so much on the specific numbers themselves, but rather on whether the scale is ticking down each day to show I'm staying disciplined.\n\nDoctor: Has your primary care doctor ever mentioned what your Body Mass Index (BMI) is, or specifically told you that your weight is dangerously low or below a healthy range for your height?\n\nPatient: I don't recall my doctor mentioning a specific BMI number, but she was definitely alarmed and made it clear she felt my weight was too low, warning me about risks like bone loss and heart issues. Honestly, those warnings feel a bit overblown to me since I still have plenty of energy to work full days and walk miles. I feel perfectly fine and active, so I just see my routine as being disciplined."

her for psychiatric evaluation despite her insistence that she was simply being careful.\n- Chief Complaint: My sense of whether I’m doing well or not has started to revolve almost entirely around what I weigh and how tightly I’m controlling my shape.\n▸ Patient-specific Symptom Manifestations\n- S043 (Restrictive_Intake): Most days I end up having black coffee for breakfast, maybe a yogurt or an apple mid‑afternoon if I can’t ignore the hunger anymore, and then a tiny salad for dinner, and I tell myself that skipping the rest is just me being efficient and “fueling optimally” for my age.\n- S044 (Low_Weight): Even though my clothes are hanging off me and my sister keeps saying I look “frail,” I still look in the mirror and think, “I could stand to tighten up a bit more,” rather than seeing anything worrisome about the number on the scale. \n- S045 (Fear_of_Weight_Gain): If I even consider having something like pasta or a sandwich, I immediately picture the extra weight showing up on my stomach the next morning and feel so anxious that it’s easier just to go without. \n- S046 (Body_Image_Weight_Shape_Disturbance): On any given day, I judge whether I’ve been a success or a failure almost entirely by whether the scale has ticked down and whether I’ve stuck exactly to my little set of food rules, more than by how my consulting work goes or how well I’m actually caring for my sister. \n- S084 (Low_Weight_Severity_Non_Recognition): When my doctor warns me about bone loss or heart issues from losing so much weight, it honestly sounds overblown to me, because I’m still walking miles through hospitals and working full days, so in my mind that proves I’m fine and just “disciplined.”\n▸ Additional Diagnostic Requirements\n- functional_impairment: I’ve started turning down longer consulting projects and evening get‑togethers with friends because I worry they’ll interfere with my eating schedule and weigh‑ins, and even when I’m with my sister at her appointments I’m often distracted, running numbers in my head about calories and weight instead of really listening.\n\n[Behavioral Guidelines]\n- In the very first turn, only talk about your chief I do know that I've lost a significant amount of weight lately, and my clothes are definitely hanging off me now.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced physical symptoms like feeling constantly cold, dizziness when standing up, or unusual hair growth on your body since you lost weight?\n\nPatient: No, I haven't really noticed any of those issues at all. her for psychiatric evaluation despite her insistence that she was simply being careful.\n- Chief Complaint: My sense of whether I’m doing well or not has started to revolve almost entirely around what I weigh and how tightly I’m controlling my shape.\n▸ Patient-specific Symptom Manifestations\n- S043 (Restrictive_Intake): Most days I end up having black coffee for breakfast, maybe a yogurt or an apple mid‑afternoon if I can’t ignore the hunger anymore, and then a tiny salad for dinner, and I tell myself that skipping the rest is just me being efficient and “fueling optimally” for my age.\n- S044 (Low_Weight): Even though my clothes are hanging off me and my sister keeps saying I look “frail,” I still look in the mirror and think, “I could stand to tighten up a bit more,” rather than seeing anything worrisome about the number on the scale. \n- S045 (Fear_of_Weight_Gain): If I even consider having something like pasta or a sandwich, I immediately picture the extra weight showing up on my stomach the next morning and feel so anxious that it’s easier just to go without. \n- S046 (Body_Image_Weight_Shape_Disturbance): On any given day, I judge whether I’ve been a success or a failure almost entirely by whether the scale has ticked down and whether I’ve stuck exactly to my little set of food rules, more than by how my consulting work goes or how well I’m actually caring for my sister. \n- S084 (Low_Weight_Severity_Non_Recognition): When my doctor warns me about bone loss or heart issues from losing so much weight, it honestly sounds overblown to me, because I’m still walking miles through hospitals and working full days, so in my mind that proves I’m fine and just “disciplined.”\n▸ Additional Diagnostic Requirements\n- functional_impairment: I’ve started turning down longer consulting projects and evening get‑togethers with friends because I worry they’ll interfere with my eating schedule and weigh‑ins, and even when I’m with my sister at her Reply naturally in English as the Patient to the last Doctor utterance.\nAnswer only the question, in 2–3 sentences maximum.\n\n[Dialogue]\nDoctor: Hello, I'm glad you're here today. What brings you in to see me?\n\nPatient: Well, my primary care doctor referred me here, though I'm still trying to understand if it's really necessary. I suppose the main issue is that my sense of whether I’m doing well or not has started to revolve almost entirely around what I weigh and how tightly I’m controlling my shape. It has just become the main way I evaluate myself lately.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever experience episodes where you eat a large amount of food in a short period while feeling like you've lost control over your eating?\n\nPatient: No, absolutely not—it's actually the complete opposite for me. I focus entirely on maintaining strict discipline and keeping tight control over my body and my rules. I restrict my food intake very carefully, so I never have moments where I lose control like that.\n\nDoctor: Could you tell me your current height and weight, and whether you have recently lost a significant amount of weight?\n\nPatient: I don't know my exact height or weight off the top of my head, but my primary care doctor has been very alarmed by my recent ongoing weight loss. Personally, I feel perfectly fine and think the concern is somewhat overblown, but she insisted I get evaluated.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed your menstrual periods becoming irregular or stopping altogether lately?\n\nPatient: Well, I'm actually 63 years old and went through menopause quite a few years ago. So, I haven't had a menstrual period in a very long time.\n\nDoctor: Do you experience an intense fear of gaining weight or becoming fat, or do you feel that some parts of your body are too fat even though others say you are underweight?\n\nPatient: Yes, if I even consider eating something like pasta or a sandwich, I get incredibly anxious that the weight will show up on my stomach the next morning. My sister constantly tells me I look \"frail\" and that my clothes are hanging off me, but when I look in the mirror, I just see areas where I could stand to tighten up. I really don't see my current weight as a problem at all.\n\nDoctor: Even if you don't know the exact numbers, could you give me a rough estimate of your current weight and height?\n\nPatient: Honestly, I'm really not sure of even a rough estimate for my height or weight. Honestly, those warnings feel a bit overblown to me since I still have plenty of energy to work full days and walk miles. I feel perfectly fine and active, so I just see my routine as being disciplined.\n\nDoctor: Do you know approximately how much weight you have lost in total, or how many clothing sizes you have dropped, since you started restricting your food?\n\nPatient: I honestly don't know the exact number of pounds or clothing sizes I've lost. I do know that my clothes are quite loose and hanging off me now, but I don't have a specific number in mind.\n\nDoctor: Do you know your height, and did your doctor mention if your weight has dropped into a range that is considered severely underweight?\n\nPatient: I'm not entirely sure of my exact height, and I don't recall if my doctor used the specific term \"severely underweight.\" While she was certainly alarmed by my weight loss and warned me about bone and heart risks, I didn't pay close attention to those specific labels because her warnings felt quite overblown to me.\n\nDoctor: Even if you don't know the exact number, do you know if your weight is currently under 100 pounds (or about 45 kilograms)?\n\nPatient: I'm really not sure of the exact number or if my weight has dropped below 100 pounds. I do know that I've lost a significant amount of weight lately, and my clothes are definitely hanging off me now.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced physical symptoms like feeling constantly cold, dizziness when standing up, or unusual hair growth on your body since you lost weight?\n\nPatient: No, I haven't really noticed any of those issues at all. I still feel like I have plenty of energy to walk miles and get through my busy workdays without feeling cold or dizzy.\n\nDoctor: Has your primary care doctor recommended any specific treatments for your weight, such as nutritional supplement drinks, a specialized eating disorder program, or hospitalization?\n\nPatient: No, my doctor hasn't recommended anything like supplement drinks, specialized programs, or hospitalization. She was much more focused on my overall weight loss and warning me about potential health risks. The responsibility amplified her sense that she needed to be \"ultra‑disciplined\" and healthy herself, and she began meticulously tracking and reducing her intake under the guise of optimizing her own longevity, fitting it neatly into her data‑driven worldview. Over several months, as consulting travel and caregiving demands increased, she found it easier to forgo meals during long stretches of work and hospital visits, subtly reshaping her routines so that eating became an afterthought and her self‑evaluation hinged more and more on how controlled she could keep her body. The issue became clinically significant when her primary care physician, alarmed by her ongoing weight loss at an annual exam and hearing her describe her strict personal \"health rules,\" referred her for psychiatric evaluation despite her insistence that she was simply being careful.\n- Chief Complaint: My sense of whether I’m doing well or not has started to revolve almost entirely around what I weigh and how tightly I’m controlling my shape.\n▸ Patient-specific Symptom Manifestations\n- S043 (Restrictive_Intake): Most days I end up having black coffee for breakfast, maybe a yogurt or an apple mid‑afternoon if I can’t ignore the hunger anymore, and then a tiny salad for dinner, and I tell myself that skipping the rest is just me being efficient and “fueling optimally” for my age.\n- S044 (Low_Weight): Even though my clothes are hanging off me and my sister keeps saying I look “frail,” I still look in the mirror and think, “I could stand to tighten up a bit more,” rather than seeing anything worrisome about the number on the scale. \n- S045 (Fear_of_Weight_Gain): If I even consider having something like pasta or a sandwich, I immediately picture the extra weight showing up on my stomach the next morning and feel so anxious that it’s easier just to go without. \n- S046 (Body_Image_Weight_Shape_Disturbance): On any given day, I judge whether I’ve been a success or a failure almost entirely by whether the scale has ticked down and whether I’ve stuck exactly to my little set of food rules, more than by how my consulting work goes or how well I’m actually caring for my sister. \n- S084 (Low_Weight_Severity_Non_Recognition): When my doctor warns me about bone loss or heart issues from losing so much weight, it honestly sounds overblown to me, because I’m still walking miles through hospitals and working full days, so in my mind that proves I’m fine and just “disciplined.”\n▸ Additional Diagnostic Requirements\n- functional_impairment: I’ve started turning down longer consulting projects and evening get‑togethers with friends because I worry they’ll interfere with my eating schedule and weigh‑ins, and even when I’m with my sister at her appointments I’m often distracted, running numbers in my head about calories and weight instead of really listening.\n\n[Behavioral Guidelines]\n- In the very first turn, only talk about your chief complaint. Do NOT reveal any other symptoms.\n- Chief complaint for the first turn: My sense of whether I’m doing well or not has started to revolve almost entirely around what I weigh and how tightly I’m controlling my shape.\n- Answer only what the doctor asks. Do not list your symptoms unprompted.\n- Speak naturally and briefly, as a real patient would (2–3 sentences max).\n- Do not mention symptoms that the doctor has not asked about.\n- If asked about a symptom not in your profile, say you are not sure.\n- Respond in English.\n\n\n[This Turn: Profile Alignment Result — Follow Strictly]\n(No match in profile or inference only)\nAnswer strategy: Since the profile does not contain specific past weight numbers or a typical stable weight, I should respond briefly and realistically, stating that I don't quite remember the exact number or didn't pay close attention to my weight back then before it became a focus."

    "content": "Below is the full consultation dialogue so far. Reply naturally in English as the Patient to the last Doctor utterance.\nAnswer only the question, in 2–3 sentences maximum.\n\n[Dialogue]\nDoctor: Hello, I'm glad you're here today. What brings you in to see me?\n\nPatient: Well, my primary care doctor referred me here, though I'm still trying to understand if it's really necessary. I suppose the main issue is that my sense of whether I’m doing well or not has started to revolve almost entirely around what I weigh and how tightly I’m controlling my shape. It has just become the main way I evaluate myself lately.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever experience episodes where you eat a large amount of food in a short period while feeling like you've lost control over your eating?\n\nPatient: No, absolutely not—it's actually the complete opposite for me. I focus entirely on maintaining strict discipline and keeping tight control over my body and my rules. I restrict my food intake very carefully, so I never have moments where I lose control like that.\n\nDoctor: Could you tell me your current height and weight, and whether you have recently lost a significant amount of weight?\n\nPatient: I don't know my exact height or weight off the top of my head, but my primary care doctor has been very alarmed by my recent ongoing weight loss. Personally, I feel perfectly fine and think the concern is somewhat overblown, but she insisted I get evaluated.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed your menstrual periods becoming irregular or stopping altogether lately?\n\nPatient: Well, I'm actually 63 years old and went through menopause quite a few years ago. So, I haven't had a menstrual period in a very long time.\n\nDoctor: Do you experience an intense fear of gaining weight or becoming fat, or do you feel that some parts of your body are too fat even though others say you are underweight?\n\nPatient: Yes, if I even consider eating something like pasta or a sandwich, I get incredibly anxious that the weight will show up on my stomach the next morning. My sister constantly tells me I look \"frail\" and that my clothes are hanging off me, but when I look in the mirror, I just see areas where I could stand to tighten up. I really don't see my current weight as a problem at all.\n\nDoctor: Even if you don't know the exact numbers, could you give me a rough estimate of your current weight and height?\n\nPatient: Honestly, I'm really not sure of even a rough estimate for my height or weight. I don't focus so much on the specific numbers themselves, but rather on whether the scale is ticking down each day to show I'm staying disciplined.\n\nDoctor: Has your primary care doctor ever mentioned what your Body Mass Index (BMI) is, or specifically told you that your weight is dangerously low or below a healthy range for your height?\n\nPatient: I don't recall my doctor mentioning a specific BMI number, but she was definitely alarmed and made it clear she felt my weight was too low, warning me about risks like bone loss and heart issues. Honestly, those warnings feel a bit overblown to me since I still have plenty of energy to work full days and walk miles. I feel perfectly fine and active, so I just see my routine as being disciplined.\n\nDoctor: Do you know approximately how much weight you have lost in total, or how many clothing sizes you have dropped, since you started restricting your food?\n\nPatient: I honestly don't know the exact number of pounds or clothing sizes I've lost. I do know that my clothes are quite loose and hanging off me now, but I don't have a specific number in mind.\n\nDoctor: Do you know your height, and did your doctor mention if your weight has dropped into a range that is considered severely underweight?\n\nPatient: I'm not entirely sure of my exact height, and I don't recall if my doctor used the specific term \"severely underweight.\" While she was certainly alarmed by my weight loss and warned me about bone and heart risks, I didn't pay close attention to those specific labels because her warnings felt quite overblown to me.\n\nDoctor: Even if you don't know the exact number, do you know if your weight is currently under 100 pounds (or about 45 kilograms)?\n\nPatient: I'm really not sure of the exact number or if my weight has dropped below 100 pounds. I do know that I've lost a significant amount of weight lately, and my clothes are definitely hanging off me now.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced physical symptoms like feeling constantly cold, dizziness when standing up, or unusual hair growth on your body since you lost weight?\n\nPatient: No, I haven't really noticed any of those issues at all. I still feel like I have plenty of energy to walk miles and get through my busy workdays without feeling cold or dizzy.\n\nDoctor: Has your primary care doctor recommended any specific treatments for your weight, such as nutritional supplement drinks, a specialized eating disorder program, or hospitalization?\n\nPatient: No, my doctor hasn't recommended anything like supplement drinks, specialized programs, or hospitalization. Instead, she just warned me about risks like bone loss and heart issues, which honestly felt quite exaggerated to me. She ultimately referred me here for this psychiatric evaluation because she was concerned about my weight loss and strict rules.\n\nDoctor: Since you mention watching the scale tick down each day to stay disciplined, what is the actual number you typically see on the scale during your daily weigh-ins?\n\nPatient: I'd really rather not focus on or share the specific number that's on the scale. For me, it's not about the exact weight itself, but just the reassurance of seeing the number tick downward to know I'm staying disciplined.\n\nDoctor: Did your primary care doctor mention how many pounds or kilograms they would like you to gain to reach what they consider a healthy weight?\n\nPatient: No, my primary care doctor didn't give me a specific number of pounds or kilograms to gain. She was much more focused on my overall weight loss and warning me about potential health risks. That is why she insisted on referring me here for this evaluation instead.\n\nDoctor: To help me understand the extent of your weight loss, do you know if you have lost more than 15% of your original body weight since you began restricting your food?\n\nPatient: I'm not entirely sure of the exact percentage of weight I've lost. I just know it must be a noticeable amount because my clothes are hanging off me and my sister keeps commenting on how frail I look.\n\nDoctor: Even if you don't know your exact height, do you have a general idea of how tall you are, perhaps in feet and inches?\n\nPatient: Honestly, I don't know my exact height and haven't had it measured in quite a long time. I couldn't even give you a reliable estimate in feet and inches.\n\nDoctor: Could you tell me what your weight was before you began restricting your food, or what your typical stable weight used to be?"

Over several months, as consulting travel and caregiving demands increased, she found it easier to forgo meals during long stretches of work and hospital visits, subtly reshaping her routines so that eating became an afterthought and her self‑evaluation hinged more and more on how controlled she could keep her body. The issue became clinically significant when her primary care physician, alarmed by her ongoing weight loss at an annual exam and hearing her describe her strict personal \"health rules,\" referred her for psychiatric evaluation despite her insistence that she was simply being careful.\n- Chief Complaint: My sense of whether I’m doing well or not has started to revolve almost entirely around age.\n- S044 (Low_Weight): Even though my clothes are hanging off me and my sister keeps saying I look “frail,” I still look in the mirror and think, “I could stand to tighten up a bit more,” rather than seeing anything worrisome about the number on the scale. \n- S045 (Fear_of_Weight_Gain): If I even consider having something like pasta or a sandwich, I immediately picture the extra weight showing up on my stomach the next morning and feel so anxious that it’s easier just to go without. \n- S046 (Body_Image_Weight_Shape_Disturbance): On any given day, I judge whether I’ve been a success or a failure almost entirely by whether the scale has ticked down and whether I’ve stuck exactly to my little set of food rules, more than by how my consulting work goes or how well I’m actually caring for my sister. \n- S084 (Low_Weight_Severity_Non_Recognition): When my doctor warns me about bone loss or heart issues from losing so much weight, it honestly sounds overblown to me, because I’m still walking miles through hospitals and working full days, so in my mind that proves I’m fine and just “disciplined.”\n▸ Additional Diagnostic Requirements\n- functional_impairment: I’ve started turning down longer consulting projects and evening get‑togethers with friends because I worry they’ll interfere with my eating schedule and weigh‑ins, and even when I’m with my sister at her Reply naturally in English as the Patient to the last Doctor utterance.\nAnswer only the question, in 2–3 sentences maximum.\n\n[Dialogue]\nDoctor: Hello, I'm glad you're here today. What brings you in to see me?\n\nPatient: Well, my primary care doctor referred me here, though I'm still trying to understand if it's really necessary. I suppose the main issue is that my sense of whether I’m doing well or not has started to revolve almost entirely around what I weigh and how tightly I’m controlling my shape. It has just become the main way I evaluate myself lately.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever experience episodes where you eat a large amount of food in a short period while feeling like you've lost control over your eating?\n\nPatient: No, absolutely not—it's actually the complete opposite for me. I focus entirely on maintaining strict discipline and keeping tight control over my body and my rules. I restrict my food intake very carefully, so I never have moments where I lose control like that.\n\nDoctor: Could you tell me your current height and weight, and whether you have recently lost a significant amount of weight?\n\nPatient: I don't know my exact height or weight off the top of my head, but my primary care doctor has been very alarmed by my recent ongoing weight loss. Personally, I feel perfectly fine and think the concern is somewhat overblown, but she insisted I get evaluated.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed your menstrual periods becoming irregular or stopping altogether lately?\n\nPatient: Well, I'm actually 63 years old and went through menopause quite a few years ago. So, I haven't had a menstrual period in a very long time.\n\nDoctor: Do you experience an intense fear of gaining weight or becoming fat, or do you feel that some parts of your body are too fat even though others say you are underweight?\n\nPatient: Yes, if I even consider eating something like pasta or a sandwich, I get incredibly anxious that the weight will show up on my stomach the next morning. My sister constantly tells me I look \"frail\" and that my clothes are hanging off me, but when I look in the mirror, I just see areas where I could stand to tighten up. I really don't see my current weight as a problem at all.\n\nDoctor: Even if you don't know the exact numbers, could you give me a rough estimate of your current weight and height?\n\nPatient: Honestly, I'm really not sure of even a rough estimate for my height or weight. I don't focus so much on the specific numbers themselves, but rather on whether the scale is ticking down each day to show I'm staying disciplined.\n\nDoctor: Has your primary care doctor ever mentioned what your Body Mass Index (BMI) is, or specifically told you that your weight is dangerously low or below a healthy range for your height?\n\nPatient: I don't recall my doctor mentioning a specific BMI number, but she was definitely alarmed and made it clear she felt my weight was too low, warning me about risks like bone loss and heart issues. Honestly, those warnings feel a bit overblown to me since I still have plenty of energy to work full days and walk miles. I feel perfectly fine and active, so I just see my routine as being disciplined.\n\nDoctor: Do you know approximately how much weight you have lost in total, or how many clothing sizes you have dropped, since you started restricting your food?\n\nPatient: I honestly don't know the exact number of pounds or clothing sizes I've lost. I do know that my clothes are quite loose and hanging off me now, but I don't have a specific number in mind.\n\nDoctor: Do you know your height, and did your doctor mention if your weight has dropped into a range that is considered severely underweight?\n\nPatient: I'm not entirely sure of my exact height, and I don't recall if my doctor used the specific term \"severely underweight.\" While she was certainly alarmed by my weight loss and warned me about bone and heart risks, I didn't pay close attention to those specific labels because her warnings felt quite overblown to me.\n\nDoctor: Even if you don't know the exact number, do you know if your weight is currently under 100 pounds (or about 45 kilograms)?\n\nPatient: I'm really not sure of the exact number or if my weight has dropped below 100 pounds. I do know that I've lost a significant amount of weight lately, and my clothes are definitely hanging off me now.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced physical symptoms like feeling constantly cold, dizziness when standing up, or unusual hair growth on your body since you lost weight?\n\nPatient: No, I haven't really noticed any of those issues at all. For me, it's not about the exact weight itself, but just the reassurance of seeing the number tick downward to know I'm staying disciplined.\n\nDoctor: Did your primary care doctor mention how many pounds or kilograms they would like you to gain to reach what they consider a healthy weight?\n\nPatient: No, my primary care doctor didn't give me a specific number of pounds or kilograms to gain. She was much more focused on my overall weight loss and warning me about potential health risks. That is why she insisted on referring me here for this evaluation instead.\n\nDoctor: To help me understand the extent of your weight loss, do you know if you have lost more than 15% of your original body weight since you began restricting your food?\n\nPatient: I'm not entirely sure of the exact percentage of weight I've lost. I just know it must be a noticeable amount because my clothes are hanging off me and my sister keeps commenting on how frail I look.\n\nDoctor: Even if you don't know your exact height, do you have a general idea of how tall you are, perhaps in feet and inches?\n\nPatient: Honestly, I don't know my exact height and haven't had it measured in quite a long time. I couldn't even give you a reliable estimate in feet and inches.\n\nDoctor: Could you tell me what your weight was before you began restricting your food, or what your typical stable weight used to be?\n\nPatient: Honestly, I don't quite remember the exact number or what my weight used to be. I didn't pay close attention to my weight back then before keeping control of my shape became such a focus for me.\n\nDoctor: Besides restricting your food, do you ever use other methods to control your weight, such as making yourself throw up, or taking laxatives, water pills, or diet pills?"

Reply naturally in English as the Patient to the last Doctor utterance.\nAnswer only the question, in 2–3 sentences maximum.\n\n[Dialogue]\nDoctor: Hello, I'm glad you're here today. What brings you in to see me?\n\nPatient: Well, my primary care doctor referred me here, though I'm still trying to understand if it's really necessary. I suppose the main issue is that my sense of whether I’m doing well or not has started to revolve almost entirely around what I weigh and how tightly I’m controlling my shape. It has just become the main way I evaluate myself lately.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever experience episodes where you eat a large amount of food in a short period while feeling like you've lost control over your eating?\n\nPatient: No, absolutely not—it's actually the complete opposite for me. I focus entirely on maintaining strict discipline and keeping tight control over my body and my rules. I restrict my food intake very carefully, so I never have moments where I lose control like that.\n\nDoctor: Could you tell me your current height and weight, and whether you have recently lost a significant amount of weight?\n\nPatient: I don't know my exact height or weight off the top of my head, but my primary care doctor has been very alarmed by my recent ongoing weight loss. Personally, I feel perfectly fine and think the concern is somewhat overblown, but she insisted I get evaluated.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed your menstrual periods becoming irregular or stopping altogether lately?\n\nPatient: Well, I'm actually 63 years old and went through menopause quite a few years ago. So, I haven't had a menstrual period in a very long time.\n\nDoctor: Do you experience an intense fear of gaining weight or becoming fat, or do you feel that some parts of your body are too fat even though others say you are underweight?\n\nPatient: Yes, if I even consider eating something like pasta or a sandwich, I get incredibly anxious that the weight will show up on my stomach the next morning. My sister constantly tells me I look \"frail\" and that my clothes are hanging off me, but when I look in the mirror, I just see areas where I could stand to tighten up. I really don't see my current weight as a problem at all.\n\nDoctor: Even if you don't know the exact numbers, could you give me a rough estimate of your current weight and height?\n\nPatient: Honestly, I'm really not sure of even a rough estimate for my height or weight. I don't focus so much on the specific numbers themselves, but rather on whether the scale is ticking down each day to show I'm staying disciplined.\n\nDoctor: Has your primary care doctor ever mentioned what your Body Mass Index (BMI) is, or specifically told you that your weight is dangerously low or below a healthy range for your height?\n\nPatient: I don't recall my doctor mentioning a specific BMI number, but she was definitely alarmed and made it clear she felt my weight was too low, warning me about risks like bone loss and heart issues. Honestly, those warnings feel a bit overblown to me since I still have plenty of energy to work full days and walk miles. I feel perfectly fine and active, so I just see my routine as being disciplined.\n\nDoctor: Do you know approximately how much weight you have lost in total, or how many clothing sizes you have dropped, since you started restricting your food?\n\nPatient: I honestly don't know the exact number of pounds or clothing sizes I've lost. I do know that my clothes are quite loose and hanging off me now, but I don't have a specific number in mind.\n\nDoctor: Do you know your height, and did your doctor mention if your weight has dropped into a range that is considered severely underweight?\n\nPatient: I'm not entirely sure of my exact height, and I don't recall if my doctor used the specific term \"severely underweight.\" While she was certainly alarmed by my weight loss and warned me about bone and heart risks, I didn't pay close attention to those specific labels because her warnings felt quite overblown to me.\n\nDoctor: Even if you don't know the exact number, do you know if your weight is currently under 100 pounds (or about 45 kilograms)?\n\nPatient: I'm really not sure of the exact number or if my weight has dropped below 100 pounds. I do know that I've lost a significant amount of weight lately, and my clothes are definitely hanging off me now.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced physical symptoms like feeling constantly cold, dizziness when standing up, or unusual hair growth on your body since you lost weight?\n\nPatient: No, I haven't really noticed any of those issues at all. I still feel like I have plenty of energy to walk miles and get through my busy workdays without feeling cold or dizzy.\n\nDoctor: Has your primary care doctor recommended any specific treatments for your weight, such as nutritional supplement drinks, a specialized eating disorder program, or hospitalization?\n\nPatient: No, my doctor hasn't recommended anything like supplement drinks, specialized programs, or hospitalization. She was much more focused on my overall weight loss and warning me about potential health risks. I just know it must be a noticeable amount because my clothes are hanging off me and my sister keeps commenting on how frail I look.\n\nDoctor: Even if you don't know your exact height, do you have a general idea of how tall you are, perhaps in feet and inches?\n\nPatient: Honestly, I don't know my exact height and haven't had it measured in quite a long time. I couldn't even give you a reliable estimate in feet and inches.\n\nDoctor: Could you tell me what your weight was before you began restricting your food, or what your typical stable weight used to be?\n\nPatient: Honestly, I don't quite remember the exact number or what my weight used to be. I didn't pay close attention to my weight back then before keeping control of my shape became such a focus for me.\n\nDoctor: Besides restricting your food, do you ever use other methods to control your weight, such as making yourself throw up, or taking laxatives, water pills, or diet pills?\n\nPatient: No, I absolutely do not use any of those methods. They are not a part of my routine at all. I prefer to rely strictly on my own self-discipline and carefully managing what I eat.\n\nDoctor: Did your primary care doctor specify whether your weight has actually dropped into the underweight range, or is it still considered within the normal range despite your recent weight loss?"

    "content": "Below is the full consultation dialogue so far. Reply naturally in English as the Patient to the last Doctor utterance.\nAnswer only the question, in 2–3 sentences maximum.\n\n[Dialogue]\nDoctor: Hello, I'm glad you're here today. What brings you in to see me?\n\nPatient: Well, my primary care doctor referred me here, though I'm still trying to understand if it's really necessary. I suppose the main issue is that my sense of whether I’m doing well or not has started to revolve almost entirely around what I weigh and how tightly I’m controlling my shape. It has just become the main way I evaluate myself lately.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever experience episodes where you eat a large amount of food in a short period while feeling like you've lost control over your eating?\n\nPatient: No, absolutely not—it's actually the complete opposite for me. I focus entirely on maintaining strict discipline and keeping tight control over my body and my rules. I restrict my food intake very carefully, so I never have moments where I lose control like that.\n\nDoctor: Could you tell me your current height and weight, and whether you have recently lost a significant amount of weight?\n\nPatient: I don't know my exact height or weight off the top of my head, but my primary care doctor has been very alarmed by my recent ongoing weight loss. Personally, I feel perfectly fine and think the concern is somewhat overblown, but she insisted I get evaluated.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed your menstrual periods becoming irregular or stopping altogether lately?\n\nPatient: Well, I'm actually 63 years old and went through menopause quite a few years ago. So, I haven't had a menstrual period in a very long time.\n\nDoctor: Do you experience an intense fear of gaining weight or becoming fat, or do you feel that some parts of your body are too fat even though others say you are underweight?\n\nPatient: Yes, if I even consider eating something like pasta or a sandwich, I get incredibly anxious that the weight will show up on my stomach the next morning. My sister constantly tells me I look \"frail\" and that my clothes are hanging off me, but when I look in the mirror, I just see areas where I could stand to tighten up. I really don't see my current weight as a problem at all.\n\nDoctor: Even if you don't know the exact numbers, could you give me a rough estimate of your current weight and height?\n\nPatient: Honestly, I'm really not sure of even a rough estimate for my height or weight. I don't focus so much on the specific numbers themselves, but rather on whether the scale is ticking down each day to show I'm staying disciplined.\n\nDoctor: Has your primary care doctor ever mentioned what your Body Mass Index (BMI) is, or specifically told you that your weight is dangerously low or below a healthy range for your height?\n\nPatient: I don't recall my doctor mentioning a specific BMI number, but she was definitely alarmed and made it clear she felt my weight was too low, warning me about risks like bone loss and heart issues. Honestly, those warnings feel a bit overblown to me since I still have plenty of energy to work full days and walk miles. I feel perfectly fine and active, so I just see my routine as being disciplined.\n\nDoctor: Do you know approximately how much weight you have lost in total, or how many clothing sizes you have dropped, since you started restricting your food?\n\nPatient: I honestly don't know the exact number of pounds or clothing sizes I've lost. I do know that my clothes are quite loose and hanging off me now, but I don't have a specific number in mind.\n\nDoctor: Do you know your height, and did your doctor mention if your weight has dropped into a range that is considered severely underweight?\n\nPatient: I'm not entirely sure of my exact height, and I don't recall if my doctor used the specific term \"severely underweight.\" While she was certainly alarmed by my weight loss and warned me about bone and heart risks, I didn't pay close attention to those specific labels because her warnings felt quite overblown to me.\n\nDoctor: Even if you don't know the exact number, do you know if your weight is currently under 100 pounds (or about 45 kilograms)?\n\nPatient: I'm really not sure of the exact number or if my weight has dropped below 100 pounds. I do know that I've lost a significant amount of weight lately, and my clothes are definitely hanging off me now.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced physical symptoms like feeling constantly cold, dizziness when standing up, or unusual hair growth on your body since you lost weight?\n\nPatient: No, I haven't really noticed any of those issues at all. I still feel like I have plenty of energy to walk miles and get through my busy workdays without feeling cold or dizzy.\n\nDoctor: Has your primary care doctor recommended any specific treatments for your weight, such as nutritional supplement drinks, a specialized eating disorder program, or hospitalization?\n\nPatient: No, my doctor hasn't recommended anything like supplement drinks, specialized programs, or hospitalization. Instead, she just warned me about risks like bone loss and heart issues, which honestly felt quite exaggerated to me. She ultimately referred me here for this psychiatric evaluation because she was concerned about my weight loss and strict rules.\n\nDoctor: Since you mention watching the scale tick down each day to stay disciplined, what is the actual number you typically see on the scale during your daily weigh-ins?\n\nPatient: I'd really rather not focus on or share the specific number that's on the scale. For me, it's not about the exact weight itself, but just the reassurance of seeing the number tick downward to know I'm staying disciplined.\n\nDoctor: Did your primary care doctor mention how many pounds or kilograms they would like you to gain to reach what they consider a healthy weight?\n\nPatient: No, my primary care doctor didn't give me a specific number of pounds or kilograms to gain. She was much more focused on my overall weight loss and warning me about potential health risks. That is why she insisted on referring me here for this evaluation instead.\n\nDoctor: To help me understand the extent of your weight loss, do you know if you have lost more than 15% of your original body weight since you began restricting your food?\n\nPatient: I'm not entirely sure of the exact percentage of weight I've lost. I just know it must be a noticeable amount because my clothes are hanging off me and my sister keeps commenting on how frail I look.\n\nDoctor: Even if you don't know your exact height, do you have a general idea of how tall you are, perhaps in feet and inches?\n\nPatient: Honestly, I don't know my exact height and haven't had it measured in quite a long time. I couldn't even give you a reliable estimate in feet and inches.\n\nDoctor: Could you tell me what your weight was before you began restricting your food, or what your typical stable weight used to be?\n\nPatient: Honestly, I don't quite remember the exact number or what my weight used to be. I didn't pay close attention to my weight back then before keeping control of my shape became such a focus for me.\n\nDoctor: Besides restricting your food, do you ever use other methods to control your weight, such as making yourself throw up, or taking laxatives, water pills, or diet pills?\n\nPatient: No, I absolutely do not use any of those methods. They are not a part of my routine at all. I prefer to rely strictly on my own self-discipline and carefully managing what I eat.\n\nDoctor: Did your primary care doctor specify whether your weight has actually dropped into the underweight range, or is it still considered within the normal range despite your recent weight loss?\n\nPatient: I don't recall my doctor using the specific terms \"underweight\" or \"normal range,\" but she was highly alarmed by my overall weight loss. She warned me about risks like bone loss, which I personally felt was an overreaction since I feel perfectly fine and active.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed a significant decrease in your sexual desire or libido since you began restricting your food?"
  }

Subtypes: Derailment or Loose Associations, Tangentiality, Incoherence or Word Salad, Neologisms, Clang Associations\n[S022] Disorganized_Abnormal_Behavior: A state where behavior and motor control are severely disturbed, appearing as movements or reactions inappropriate to the situation.  Subtypes: Childlike Silliness, Stupor, Catalepsy, Waxy Flexibility, Mutism, Negativism, Posturing, Mannerism, Stereotypy, Agitation, Grimacing, Echolalia, Echopraxia\n[S023] Negative_Symptoms: A state where emotions, motivation, speech, or social interest that should normally be present are diminished or lost.  Subtypes: Diminished Emotional Expression, Avolition, Alogia, Anhedonia, Asociality\n[S024] Depressed_Mood: A state where a sad, empty, or hopeless mood persists.  Subtypes: Depressed Mood, Tearfulness, Sadness, Emptiness, Emotional Numbness\n[S025] Loss_of_Interest: A state where interest or pleasure in previously enjoyed activities is markedly diminished.  Subtypes: Loss of Interest, Social Withdrawal, Loss of Motivation, Anhedonia, Indifference to Achievement\n[S026] Weight_Appetite_Change: Significant increase or decrease in appetite or weight.  Subtypes: Significant Weight Loss, Weight Gain, Decrease in Appetite, Increase in Appetite\n[S027] Sleep_Disturbance: A state where there is a significant change in the quantity or quality of sleep.  Subtypes: Initial Insomnia, Middle Insomnia, Early Morning Awakening, Hypersomnia, Nightmare Disorder, Non-Restorative Sleep\n[S028] Psychomotor_Changes: Observable increase or decrease in psychomotor activity.  Subtypes: Psychomotor Agitation, Psychomotor Retardation (Motor), Psychomotor Retardation (Speech)\n[S029] Fatigue: Persistent fatigue that is not relieved by rest and a marked lack of energy.  Subtypes: Chronic Fatigue, Physical Fatigue, Mental Fatigue, Loss of Energy\n[S030] Worthlessness_Guilt: Feelings of worthlessness or excessive or inappropriate guilt (which may be delusional; not merely self-reproach or guilt about being sick).  Subtypes: Feelings of Worthlessness, Ruminative Guilt, Excessive or Inappropriate Guilt, Delusional Guilt\n[S031] Diminished_Thinking: Diminished ability to think or concentrate, or indecisiveness.  Subtypes: Reduced Concentration, Indecisiveness, Subjective Memory Complaints, Clouded Thinking\n[S032] Death_Suicide: Recurrent thoughts of death, recurrent suicidal ideation, or a suicide attempt. Subtypes: Elevated Mood, Irritable Mood, Mood Lability\n[S034] Increased_Activity: Abnormally and persistently increased goal-directed activity or energy. Subtypes: Talkativeness, Rapid Speech, Pressured Speech\n[S038] Racing_Thoughts: Flight of ideas or subjective experience that thoughts are racing.  Subtypes: Racing Thoughts, Flooding of Thoughts, Inability to Control Thoughts\n[S039] Manic_Distractibility: Attention is too easily drawn to unimportant or irrelevant external stimuli.  Subtypes: Responsiveness to Extraneous Stimuli, Frequent Task Switching, Inability to Sustain Attention\n[S040] Increased_Goal_Activity: Increase in goal-directed activity (at work, school, socially, or sexually) or purposeless psychomotor agitation.  Subtypes: Occupational Hyperactivity, Social Hyperactivity, Hypersexuality, Psychomotor Agitation\n[S041] Painful_Consequence_Behavior: Excessive involvement in activities that have a high potential for painful consequences.  Subtypes: Unrestrained Buying Sprees, Foolish Business Investments, Sexual Indiscretions, Impulsive Risky Behavior\n[S042] Low_Self_Esteem: A persistent tendency to evaluate oneself as incompetent or worthless; chronically low self-evaluation.  Subtypes: Feelings of Worthlessness, Self-Criticism, Reduced Self-Efficacy, Chronic Sense of Inferiority, Lack of Self-Assurance\n[S043] Restrictive_Intake: Persistent restriction of energy intake relative to requirements, leading to significantly low body weight.  Subtypes: Eating Very Small Amounts, Skipping Meals, Exclusion of Specific Food Groups, Strict Calorie Restriction\n[S044] Low_Weight: A body weight that is significantly below the minimum level of health for age, sex, developmental trajectory, and physical health.  Subtypes: Continuous Weight Loss, Insistence on Low Weight\n[S045] Fear_of_Weight_Gain: Intense fear of gaining weight or of becoming fat, even though at a significantly low weight.  Subtypes: Fear of Weight Gain, Fear of Specific Foods, Obsession with Weight Gain\n[S046] Body_Image_Weight_Shape_Disturbance: Undue influence of body weight or shape on self-evaluation.  Subtypes: Distorted Body Image, Self-Worth Dependent on Weight\n[S047] Compensatory_Behavior_NonPurging: Recurrent inappropriate compensatory behaviors to prevent weight gain without purging (direct removal of food).  Subtypes: Fasting, Excessive Exercise\n[S048] Compensatory_Behavior_Purging: Recurrent inappropriate compensatory behaviors involving the expulsion of food or fluids to prevent weight gain.  Subtypes: Self-Induced Vomiting, Misuse of Medications\n[S049] Eating_Large_Amount: Eating, in a discrete period of time, an amount of food that is definitely larger than what most individuals would eat in a similar period of time under similar circumstances.\n[S050] Loss_of_Control: A sense of lack of control over eating during the episode (e.g., a feeling that one cannot stop eating or control what or how much one is eating).\n[S051] Rapid_Eating: Eating much more rapidly than normal.\n[S052] Eating_Until_Uncomfortably_Full: Eating until feeling uncomfortably full.\n[S053] Eating_Without_Hunger: Eating large amounts of food when not feeling physically hungry.\n[S054] Eating_Alone_Due_to_Embarrassment: Eating alone because of feeling embarrassed by how much one is eating.\n[S055] Negative_Affect_After_Binge: Feeling disgusted with oneself, depressed, or very guilty afterward.\n[S084] Low_Weight_Severity_Non_Recognition: Persistent lack of recognition of the seriousness of the current low body weight. Subtypes: Feeling Keyed Up, Physical Tension, Nervousness\n[S059] Concentration_Difficulty: Difficulty concentrating or mind going blank.  Subtypes: Interference by Worry, Inability to Sustain Attention, Mind Going Blank\n[S060] Irritability: A state of reacting sensitively or sharply to minor stimuli.  Subtypes: Low Frustration Tolerance, Snappiness, Exhausted Patience\n[S061] Muscle_Tension: A state where muscles are continuously tense, stiff, and accompanied by discomfort.  Subtypes: Neck and Shoulder Tension, Jaw Tension, Generalized Muscle Aches, Chronic Stiffness\n[S062] Specific_Phobic_Fear: Marked fear or anxiety about a specific object or situation that is out of proportion to the actual danger. Subtypes: Animal Type, Natural Environment Type, Blood-Injection-Injury Type, Situational Type, Other Type\n[S063] Immediate_Fear/Anxiety_Response: The phobic object or situation almost always provokes immediate fear or anxiety. Subtypes: Contamination Obsessions, Pathological Doubt, Symmetry and Exactness Obsessions, Forbidden or Taboo Thoughts, Somatic Obsessions\n[S066] Compulsions: Repetitive behaviors or mental acts performed in response to an obsession or to prevent anxiety/distress; clearly excessive or not realistically connected.  Subtypes: Cleaning or Washing Compulsions, Checking Compulsions, Ordering and Arranging Compulsions, Mental Acts, Repeating Rituals\n[S067] Preoccupation_with_Appearance: Preoccupation with one or more perceived defects or flaws in physical appearance that are not observable or appear slight to others.  Subtypes: Skin Defects, Facial Features, Asymmetry, Muscle Dysmorphia, Specific Body Parts\n[S068] Appearance_Related_Repetitive_Behaviors: Repetitive behaviors or mental acts performed in response to appearance concerns. Subtypes: Intrusive Memories, Distressing Dreams, Dissociative Reactions (Flashbacks), Psychological Distress at Cues, Physiological Reactions to Cues\n[S070] Trauma_Avoidance: Persistent avoidance of stimuli associated with the traumatic event.  Subtypes: Internal Avoidance, External Avoidance\n[S071] Trauma_Related_Amnesia: Inability to remember an important aspect of the traumatic event (typically due to dissociative amnesia and not to other factors like head injury or alcohol).  Subtypes: Fragmented Memory, Generalized Amnesia\n[S072] Negative_Beliefs: Persistent and exaggerated negative beliefs or expectations about oneself, others, or the world.  Subtypes: Negative Self-Beliefs, Negative Beliefs about Others, Negative Worldview\n[S073] Distorted_Cognitions_With_Blame: Persistent, distorted cognitions about the cause or consequences of the traumatic event that lead the individual to blame himself/herself or others.
